     1. **6q-4 runs as two slices.** 6q-4a builds the editor: the colours, the file types, find and replace, Ctrl+S with its snapshot and Undo, drafts, the reload of a file that changed on disk, and a bar with Keep mine and Take disk when the tab has unsaved edits. 6q-4b adds the three-way merge to that bar. Each has its own plan, live check and pull request. One slice was the other option.
     2. **Unsaved edits are kept as drafts.** A draft is stored in Marshell's home and survives a session switch, a tab that unloads and a restart, as Sublime Text's hot exit does. The tab shows a dot while its file has unsaved edits. Closing such a tab asks Save, Don't save or Cancel. Quitting Marshell doesn't ask. Keeping the edits in memory until quit, and saving on its own after a pause, were the other options.
     3. **A file that changes on disk under unsaved edits gets a bar.** It reads "<name> changed on disk." and offers Merge, Keep mine and Take disk. Merge folds the disk's changes into the person's text, with the text last loaded or saved as the base; lines changed on both sides get conflict markers in the text, and Ctrl+Z undoes the merge. 6q-4a ships the bar with Keep mine and Take disk, and 6q-4b adds Merge. A merge that applies on its own when nothing conflicts, and a side-by-side compare (which needs `@codemirror/merge`), were the other options.
     4. **Builders are as in 6q-3.** The main thread writes the save route, its refusals and its snapshots, and in 6q-4b the merge. Sonnet workers take the frontend and the mechanical tasks, each checked in the main thread. Codex CLI, and no workers, were the other options.
     5. **Three extras are in.** Typing a bracket or a quote adds its closing one. Ctrl+G goes to a line. After a file that changed on disk reloads, the gutter marks the lines that changed, until the person edits or leaves the tab. Bracket matching and a word-wrap switch were stated with the question as part of the set. Several cursors (Ctrl+D, Alt+click) were offered and stay out.
     6. **A Markdown or HTML file's Rendered view shows the editor's text,** saved or not. Its Source view is the editor. Showing the saved file was the other option.
     7. **The user approved CodeMirror 6 (SCOPE.md, dependencies):** `@codemirror/state`, `view`, `commands`, `search`, `language` and `autocomplete` (which holds the closing of brackets), with the small packages they need. They are MIT-licensed, make no network request, and get their notices in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`. `@codemirror/merge` is not added.
     8. **Undo covers the last save.** After a save the tab reads "Saved <name>." with an Undo button, and the palette has "Undo last save" while the tab stays open. Undo puts the file back as it was before the save, and the editor keeps the person's text. Each file keeps its last 20 snapshots, as a config file does. A list of earlier versions was the other option.
     9. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **Every text kind opens in the editor:** code, plain text, and the Source view of Markdown and HTML. Images and PDFs stay viewers. A file that isn't text, or is over 2 MiB, shows the line it shows today.
        - **A save writes the file in place,** as editors do, after its snapshot is on disk. The file keeps its permissions, its hard links and its place for a program that watches it. The config service keeps its temp file and rename. The research note proposed the rename for user files too; a rename drops a hard link and a file's own permissions, and some watchers lose the file.
        - **The save is refused** for a file in Marshell's own folder, for a file an organization manages (the config guard's names and folders), and for every path the pane can't read (UNC and device paths, and links to them). Such a file opens read-only with a line that says why, and so does a file marked read-only on disk. A CLI's own settings file saves at once like any other: the edit is the person's. "Config writes keep their preview" (second round 7) is about the changes Marshell itself proposes.
        - **A save is checked against the bytes that were loaded,** by their SHA-256. When the file on disk is no longer those bytes, nothing is written and the bar shows. Undo is checked the same way: it runs only while the file still holds what the save wrote.
        - **Snapshots of saves are kept apart from the config service's,** in their own folder of Marshell's home, so the Doctor's undo list shows only config changes. At most 256 MiB of them are kept; the oldest go first.
        - **A draft belongs to its file, not to a tab.** The same file in two sessions shows one draft, and opening a file again brings its draft back. A draft goes when the file is saved, when the person answers Don't save or Take disk, or when the text is back to what the disk holds. A draft that no tab shows is dropped 30 days after its last edit. Closing a session doesn't ask about its unsaved tabs: the drafts stay.
        - **The encoding and the line endings are kept.** UTF-8 with or without a byte-order mark and UTF-16 with one open and save as they are. A file with both kinds of line ending is saved with the kind it has most of. The tab's footer shows the cursor's line and column, the encoding and the line ending.
        - **The app's shortcuts work from the editor,** as from the Address box ("Slice 6q-3 decisions" 13). Ctrl+Shift+K, Ctrl+Shift+L and the others keep their meaning, and the editor's own commands on those keys are left out. Alt+Left and Alt+Right stay the tab's Back and Forward. Esc doesn't leave the editor: it closes the find bar, and Esc then Tab leaves (second round 8). Ctrl+Shift+Tab goes back to the terminal as from any tab. Coming the other way, Ctrl+Shift+Tab puts the keyboard in the editor of a text tab, at the cursor, not on the tab's body. Esc arms the Tab that leaves every time, also when it only dropped a selection.
        - **Word wrap** is on for Markdown and plain text and off for code, a script that only its first line names as code included. Alt+Z switches it for the tab.
        - **The colours** are one set of `--code-*` tokens, light and dark, each at least 4.5:1 on the pane's surfaces and held there by the contrast test. Every place that colours code in the pane uses them, a Markdown file's code blocks included; they no longer follow the terminal's theme. In forced colours the text is the system's and nothing is coloured.
        - **Colouring runs in small steps,** the lines on screen first, and typing never waits for it. A file over 1 MiB, and a line over 2,000 characters, stay plain. The live check measured it inside WebView2 and the limit stays: a 1 MiB TypeScript file (46,764 lines) showed its first colours 76 ms after its tab was selected and had its last line coloured 2.7 s after a jump to the end, a 300 KB one took 48 ms and 0.8 s, and typing at the end of each, and of a 2 MiB file without colours, kept every frame under 11 ms.
        - **A file's type** comes from its extension, then its exact name (`Makefile`, `Dockerfile`, `.gitignore`), then its first line (`#!/usr/bin/env python`). A type Shiki has no grammar for opens plain.
        - **Screen readers** get the automated checks in this slice. The NVDA pass the research note asked for runs at the release sign-off, with the editor added to `docs/a11y/screen-reader-script.md` (the user's rule of 2026-10-17).
        - **Dragging selected text to move it doesn't work on Windows,** because native file drop turns HTML5 drag and drop off there. It is a known limit.
     10. **What the live check changed (2026-11-18).** The note is `docs/research/2026-11-18-text-editor-live-check.md`.
        - **Undo of a save leaves the editor's text,** as decision 8 says: the file goes back, and the tab is unsaved again with a draft. The plan's task had the editor take the old text when it had no newer edits.
        - **After Undo, Keep mine, Take disk, and a close answered with Save,** the keyboard stays in the pane: in the editor, or on the tab's body in Rendered view.
        - **Ctrl+S while the bar is up writes nothing and says so:** "<name> changed on disk, so it wasn't saved."
        - **A file with no draft answers 204,** not 404, which a webview logs as an error for every file opened.
        - **The go-to-line box's button reads "Go".**
        - **Left as they are:** with a browser tab open, Tab from the window's last control leaves the document for one press before it comes round; the footer's hint is the same in a read-only editor, where Tab alone leaves; a link followed from a tab with unsaved edits asks nothing, and the draft waits for Back.
     11. **What the final review changed (2026-11-19).** Five findings were fixed and checked again in the app; the note has them.
        - **A file has one editor in a session.** Two tabs can come to show one file (a link followed in one of them, Back, Forward). Only one of them is drawn at a time: the selected one, and the other opens on the draft it left. Before, each kept its own text and they wrote over one draft, so one tab's edits could go without the close question.
        - **A draft is shown for a file that is now read-only or gone.** A read-only file opens with its draft's text in the read-only editor, under the read-only line, and keeps its dot. A file that was already gone when its tab opened opens the editor on its draft, under "<name> was deleted on disk. Ctrl+S saves it again.", and a save writes it the way the draft says it was stored. Without a draft the tab still says "This file was deleted."
        - **A read of the disk waits while a save or an undo is in flight,** and is taken when that is answered. Before, a check that landed during an undo put the old text in the editor against decision 8, and one that landed during a save could raise the bar over the tab's own write.
        - **A reload is spoken only to someone whose keyboard is in the tab.** Its line still shows under the text. A file an agent writes every second no longer talks over the terminal; this is the rule the pane's "Updated" note already had.
        - **A close asked from the tab strip leaves the keyboard on the strip,** as a close that asks nothing does, so a second Delete closes the next tab instead of deleting a character in its file.
        - **A save that fails leaves the file as it was** (from the review before the merge). The file is opened for writing before its old text is copied, so a file another program holds costs no copy however often the save is tried. A write that is cut short, as on a full disk, puts the old text back over what it wrote; if that fails too, the old text is still in the file history.
        - **A tab that takes a file over waits for the other tab's draft** (same review). The tab that is drawn for a file reads the draft only after every draft request for that file has been answered, so it can't open on a text older than the one just typed.
        - **A draft may be as long as a UTF-16 file at the limit** (same review): 3 MB of text, since a draft is kept as UTF-8 and a character that takes two bytes in the file can take three there. Before, a UTF-16 file over about 1.3 MB of East Asian text could be edited and saved but never kept as a draft.
        - **Left as they are:** a draft for a file that is now over 2 MB or no longer text can't be read in the app (the close question still asks, and Don't save drops it); a clean tab whose file is deleted and written again between two checks shows the bar where a reload would do; a file another program has locked against reading answers "That path can't be saved."; text pasted with half a surrogate pair is saved as it is, and a UTF-16 file then reads as not text; app shortcuts don't fire from the find field; a hard link into Marshell's own folder is saved like any file.
   - **Slice 6q-4b decisions (2026-11-19).** The three-way merge on the "changed on disk" bar. Asked of the user on 2026-11-19, when the slice was planned (1 to 4); 5 is what the plan settled without asking. They build on "Slice 6q-4 decisions" 3. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-19-phase-6q-4b-editor-merge.md`.
     1. **A conflict has two parts.** Lines changed both by the person and on disk read `<<<<<<< mine`, the person's lines, `=======`, the disk's lines, `>>>>>>> disk`. Three parts, with the lines as they were when the file was loaded under `||||||| before`, was the other option.
     2. **The editor tints a conflict and jumps between conflicts.** The marker lines and the two sides are tinted, the status line gives the count, and "Next conflict" and "Previous conflict" are commands with keys and palette entries. A conflict is resolved by editing the text. Markers only, and buttons on each conflict (Take mine, Take disk, Take both), were the other options.
     3. **Ctrl+S saves a text that still has conflicts, and says so:** "Saved <name>. 2 conflicts left." Undo of the save works as for any save. Asking first was the other option.
     4. **Ctrl+Z after a merge brings the bar back,** with Merge, Keep mine and Take disk as before the merge. Ctrl+Y redoes the merge and the bar goes. The bar staying gone, as after Keep mine, was the other option.
     5. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **The merge runs in the core,** in the function phase 3b built on `diffy` (3b decision 6), which moves from `config` to `userfiles` and gains the two labels. The route takes three texts and no path, and writes nothing.
        - **A merge has limits, because `diffy` slows with the square of the lines that changed.** Measured on the development PC in a release build: a 20,000-line file rewritten whole merges in 1.2 s, and a 50,000-line one would take about 8 s; a development build is about 26 times slower. So each side may differ from the base by at most 20,000 lines (lines gone plus lines new), a merge is given 10 seconds, one merge runs at a time, each text is at most 3 MB, and so is the result. A merge past a limit is refused with "<name> couldn't be merged." and the reason, and the bar stays.
        - **The bar's buttons are Merge, Keep mine and Take disk,** in that order (6q-4 decision 3). After a merge the keyboard is in the editor, the cursor is on the first conflict, and the gutter marks the lines the merge changed, as after a reload. The line under the text reads "Merged <name>. 2 conflicts to resolve. F7 goes to the next conflict, and Ctrl+Z undoes the merge.", or "Merged <name>. No conflicts. Ctrl+Z undoes the merge."
        - **F7 is Next conflict and Shift+F7 is Previous conflict,** in the editor only; neither is an app shortcut. Each goes round at the end, and says "Conflict 2 of 3." or "<name> has no conflicts."
        - **Any conflict in a text is tinted and counted,** git's own included (`<<<<<<< HEAD`, and the three-part kind): a file an agent left conflicted reads the same way. A conflict is a line of seven `<`, then one of seven `=`, then one of seven `>`, in that order. The tints stop above 1 MiB of text, as the syntax colours do; the jumps and the count do not.
        - **The person may type while a merge runs.** An answer for a text that has since changed, or for a file that changed again, is dropped and the merge runs again on what is there now.
        - **The bar comes back by the text, not by the editor's history.** While the tab is open, the text as it was before the merge is known to rest on the file as it was loaded, and the merged text on the file as it was merged. Whichever of the two the editor holds decides what the next save is checked against and whether the bar shows; so the bar also comes back after edits that are undone past the merge, and when the file changed again in between it shows that newer file. A save, Keep mine, Take disk and a reload end this.
        - **Merge is there for every tab that shows the bar:** also for a read-only file whose draft is shown, and in a Markdown or HTML file's Rendered view, where the markers show as text. Since decision 7: not in a read-only tab, and a merge with conflicts in the Rendered view shows the Source view.
        - **Builders** are as 6q-4 decision 4 says: the main thread writes the merge and its route, Sonnet workers the frontend.
     6. **Settled while building, and what the live check found** (`docs/research/2026-11-19-editor-merge-live-check.md`; all eleven checks passed and the code was not changed by it).
        - **A merge is wanted only while the bar is up.** However the bar goes (Keep mine, Take disk, a deleted file, a disk that comes to hold the editor's text or goes back to what was loaded, the disk's own text typed by hand, a redo of an earlier merge), the merge that was running is over: its answer is dropped when it comes, and a bar that comes up later is not merged unasked. Each request has a number and only the answer to the last one counts, so an answer to a request that was given up can't end or restart a later one. An answer that comes after the tab went is dropped too, so it can't write back a draft the person chose not to keep. Decision 5 dropped an answer only when the bar was down at the moment it came.
        - **A merge is one undo step of its own.** The editor's history joins changes made within half a second, and a merge joined to the last keys would make Ctrl+Z pass the text from before the merge, which is the text that brings the bar back. The same holds for a reload and for Take disk. While a merge runs and the person types, the line under the text keeps reading "Merging <name>…".
        - **The jump after a merge is quiet.** The cursor goes to the first conflict, and the keyboard stays where the person has it, because the answer can come seconds after the press; the "Merged …" line is what is announced. F7, Shift+F7 and the two palette commands announce the place, and the palette's two put the keyboard in the editor.
        - **In the Rendered view the two palette commands say "Conflicts show in the Source view."** and move nothing; the editor is hidden there.
        - **The tints are translucent,** so a selection, which the editor draws behind its lines, shows through: 16% of a blue-green for the person's lines, 16% of an amber for the disk's, 24% of a grey for the marker lines and 12% of it for a base section. Every code colour keeps 4.5:1 on each (the lowest is 4.56:1, a comment on a marker line in light). In forced colours a conflict's lines have a 3 px `CanvasText` bar at their start and no tint.
        - **A conflict's second `=======` line is text.** After the middle line, a further `=======` or `|||||||` line belongs to the disk's side, and an end line with no middle line before it ends nothing.
        - **The bar is reached from the editor with Esc and then Shift+Tab,** as since 6q-4a: it is above the editor. Quick presses of F7 are announced at most once in 1.5 seconds, by the announcer's own limit.
     7. **The user's answers after the merge (2026-11-19).** Two calls PR #142 left as known limits, and a third found while fixing them. They change decision 5's "Merge is there for every tab that shows the bar". Live check: `docs/research/2026-11-19-editor-merge-calls-live-check.md`.
        1. **A read-only tab's bar has no Merge,** only Keep mine and Take disk. Such a tab can't keep the merged text, and its editor takes no Ctrl+Z, so "Ctrl+Z undoes the merge." was untrue there. Keeping Merge and dropping the Ctrl+Z part, or letting Ctrl+Z undo a merge in a read-only tab, were the other options. A merge already running when the file becomes read-only isn't taken either: its line says the file "became read-only while the merge ran" (after the PR #143 review). A keyboard on Merge when it goes moves to Keep mine.
        2. **A merge that leaves conflicts in the Rendered view shows the Source view.** The tab switches to Source as the answer comes, and the cursor goes to the first conflict. A keyboard on the tab's body, where pressing Merge put it, goes on into the editor, where F7 and Ctrl+Z work; a keyboard the person moved elsewhere stays there, so this view switch, unlike one the person makes, doesn't move the keyboard to the tab's body. "On the tab's body" includes the rendered text inside it, such as a link. The jump belongs to the draw that brings the merge's answer: a tab whose switch didn't come then (it stays in Rendered) doesn't jump later, when the person shows the Source view (after the PR #143 review). A different line, or F7 handled in the Rendered view, were the other options.
        3. **A clean merge in the Rendered view stays there,** and its line reads "Merged <name>. No conflicts." without "Ctrl+Z undoes the merge.", because the Rendered view has no undo; the Source view's editor has it. Switching to Source after every merge, or keeping the line, were the other options.
   - **Slice 6q-5 decisions (2026-11-19).** Find, an outline and page thumbnails in a PDF tab, and the PDF's own links. Asked of the user on 2026-11-19, when the slice was planned (1 to 7), and after a UX pass of the plan the same day (9 and 10); 8 and 11 are what the plan settled without asking. They build on "Slice 6q decisions", first round 9 and second round 1. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-19-phase-6q-5-pdf-find-outline.md`.
     1. **The outline and the page thumbnails share one sidebar.** A toolbar button opens it at the tab's left edge with two views, Outline and Pages. It pushes the pages over; in a tab under 560 px wide it lies over them and closes after a pick. It is closed when the tab opens. (Since decision 10: it opens as the person last left it.) A button for each view, and an outline menu with a grid of pages that fills the tab, were the other options.
     2. **The find bar is the editor's, without Replace and regular expressions.** It sits at the top, under the toolbar: the field, the count ("3 of 17"), Previous, Next, Match case and Whole word. Every match is tinted, and the current one more strongly. Enter and Shift+Enter, or F3 and Shift+F3, step through the matches, and Esc closes the bar. A Match diacritics switch, and a bar with no switches, were the other options.
     3. **A PDF's links work.** A link to a place in the file jumps there. A web link (http or https) opens a browser tab in the side pane, as Ctrl+click on an address in terminal output does. Any other kind (mailto:, a link to another file) does nothing and says why. Links within the file only, and no links, were the other options.
     4. **Builders are as in 6q-4.** The main thread writes find: the match mapping and the highlights on the text layer. Sonnet workers take the sidebar, the links and the mechanical tasks, each checked in the main thread. Codex CLI, and no workers, were the other options.
     5. **A jump inside a PDF joins the tab's Back and Forward.** An outline entry, a thumbnail, a link and the Page field each record the place left in the tab's history, and Back (Alt+Left) returns to it, as in Edge and Acrobat. Scrolling records nothing. Jumps from links and the outline only, and no jumps in the history, were the other options.
     6. **F4 shows and hides the sidebar** while the keyboard is in a PDF tab, and puts the keyboard in it, as Acrobat's navigation-pane key does. The palette has "Show PDF outline" and "Show PDF pages". The palette and Tab alone, and an app chord, were the other options.
     7. **The outline follows the scroll.** The section that holds the page in view is marked, and its parents open so it shows. When the sidebar opens, the keyboard lands on that entry. An outline that stays as the person left it was the other option.
     8. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **The tab keeps its own page renderer,** and pdf.js's find controller (`PDFFindController` in `pdfjs-dist/web/pdf_viewer.mjs`, already shipped) searches it. The highlights and the links are Marshell's own code on its pages. The move to pdf.js's `PDFViewer` or `PDFPageView` waits for 6q-6, which needs it for notes (option C in `docs/research/2026-11-07-tabs-pane-options.md` §2.6).
        - **The text layer is built without normalization,** as pdf.js's own viewer builds it, so a match is tinted on the text it was found in.
        - **A place in a PDF is a page and how far down it.** A history entry keeps it, saved with the tab, so Back from another file to a PDF returns to the page left too. A tab that unloads, and a restart, open the PDF at the place last recorded, or else at page 1. A destination that names a height puts that height at the top of the view; one that names none puts the page's top there.
        - **The sidebar is 200 px wide.** It opens on the view last chosen (decision 10), and the first time on Outline when the PDF has one, or else on Pages. The Outline view of a PDF without one reads "This PDF has no outline." with a "Show pages" button.
        - **Keys in the sidebar.** F4 again and Esc put the keyboard back on the pages, and Esc also closes a sidebar that lies over them. Enter on an entry or a thumbnail jumps: the keyboard stays in a sidebar beside the pages, and goes to the pages when the sidebar closes after the pick. The outline is a tree: Up and Down move, Right opens an entry or goes to its first child, Left closes it or goes to its parent, Home and End go to the ends. The thumbnails are a list: Up, Down, Home, End, Page Up and Page Down.
        - **Thumbnails are drawn one at a time,** those in the sidebar's view first, at its width. The page in view is marked.
        - **Find searches every page and tints the drawn ones.** Ctrl+F opens the bar with the text selected in the PDF (one line at most), or else with the last search. F3 with no search opens the bar. The bar stays open over a reload and searches the new file, tinting its matches without moving the view; the next step goes from the page in view (live check, 2026-11-19). A PDF with no text says "This PDF has no text to search. Its pages may be scanned images." A step is announced: "Match 3 of 17, page 5.", with "Continued from the start." or "Continued from the end." after a wrap.
        - **Links are drawn on the pages that are drawn,** and each is a Tab stop. A plain click follows one. A link's named actions next, previous, first and last page are jumps, and its Back and Forward are the tab's. Any other link says "Only links within the file and web links open here."
        - **Dev serves pdf.js's JavaScript as JavaScript.** The Vite plugin sent every `/pdfjs/` file except `.wasm` as `application/octet-stream`, so the decoders pdf.js loads for JBIG2 and JPEG 2000 images could fail in `pnpm tauri dev`. The live check opens a scanned JBIG2 or JPEG 2000 PDF in dev and in a release build.
        - **The palette's three PDF commands,** "Find in PDF", "Show PDF outline" and "Show PDF pages", are listed only while a loaded PDF tab is on screen, and have no app key.
     9. **The toolbar fits a 320 px pane with icons.** Zoom out, Zoom in and Fit width become icon buttons that keep their names and tooltips, like the header's Back, Forward and Select region. The order is the sidebar button, the Page field, the zoom group, and Find last. Under 360 px the word "Page" hides, and the field keeps its name for screen readers. Text buttons wrapping to a second row, and a ⋯ menu for zoom, were the other options.
     10. **The sidebar follows the person's last choice.** A PDF tab opens with the sidebar as it was last left in any PDF tab, open or closed and on Outline or Pages, while Marshell runs. It never opens by itself where it would lie over the pages, and after a restart it starts closed. Closed every time, and a setting kept across restarts, were the other options.
     11. **The UX pass's other calls (2026-11-19), not asked.**
        - **The sidebar button's icon is a panel with its left part filled,** not three lines, which are the app's Menu button.
        - **A match on the page looks like a highlighter:** a yellow for every match and a deeper orange for the current one, the same in both themes because the page is always white paper, and blended so the print under it stays dark. In forced colours a match is the system's Highlight with HighlightText print, and the current match also has a 2 px HighlightText ring set inside it (a Highlight ring would not show on the Highlight fill).
        - **Outline and Pages switch on a filled track,** the look of the Rendered and Source toggle, with text labels.
        - **An outline entry shows its page number at its end,** in the quieter text colour, and its title wraps instead of being cut. The arrow opens and closes an entry, and a click anywhere else on it jumps.
        - **A thumbnail has its page number under it,** and a page not drawn yet is plain white paper of the page's shape.
        - **A sidebar that lies over the pages** has the raised surface and a shadow, with no dimming behind it. It closes when a click or the keyboard goes anywhere outside it but its own button. Switching to another window keeps it open, with the keyboard in it when the window comes back (PR review, 2026-11-19).
        - **The sidebar opens and closes at once,** without motion, because the pages fit their new width.
        - **A change of scale keeps the reading place.** Zoom, and fit width following the sidebar or the pane's width, keep the place at the top of the view where it was.
        - **A jump, and Back or Forward within the same PDF, are announced:** "Page 12 of 140." A jump to the place already in view moves nothing and says the page all the same (PR review, 2026-11-19). The page said is the one the Page field then shows, the last page whose top has passed the upper third of the view, so a place near the end of a page reads as the next page (live check, 2026-11-19).
        - **The find bar closes back to where the keyboard was** when it opened: the pages, the sidebar or the toolbar. Like the editor's bar, it wraps: Match case and Whole word go to a second row when one row doesn't fit.
        - **A web link's tooltip shows its address.** A link shows a pointer and a faint tint under the pointer, and a focus ring for the keyboard.
        - **The Find and sidebar buttons name their keys** to screen readers (`aria-keyshortcuts` "Control+F" and "F4").
        - **The sidebar's lists scroll with the themed scrollbar,** as the PDF's pages do.
   - **Slice 6q-6 decisions (2026-11-19).** Notes in a PDF tab: highlights, comments on them, and free text, saved into the file. Asked of the user on 2026-11-19, when the slice was planned. They build on "Slice 6q decisions", first round 9 and second round 9, and on "Slice 6q-5 decisions". The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-19-phase-6q-6-pdf-notes.md`.
     1. **A note is made from a selection or with a tool.** Selecting text on a page shows a small bar beside it with Highlight and Comment. The toolbar also has a Highlight tool and a Free text tool. By keyboard, Ctrl+F finds the words, and the find bar's Highlight and Comment buttons mark the current match. Tools alone, as in Firefox, and the selection bar alone, were the other options.
     2. **Highlights come in pdf.js's five colours,** yellow first, then green, blue, pink and red. The colour last picked is the next highlight's while Marshell runs. Yellow only, and the five plus any colour, were the other options.
     3. **A comment is typed in a small popup by its note, and a Notes view lists them all.** Notes is a third view in the sidebar, beside Outline and Pages. It lists every note in page order with its comment, and Enter jumps to one, so the keyboard and screen readers reach every note in one place. Popups on the page only, and comments typed in the sidebar, were the other options.
     4. **Ctrl+S saves the notes into the file, and unsaved notes are kept as a draft,** as in a text tab. A dot marks a tab with unsaved notes. A save keeps a backup first and offers Undo. Unsaved notes survive quitting, closing the session and hiding the pane, kept in Marshell's home. Closing the one tab asks first, as it does for a text tab (`closeAsk`; this line was settled with the plan, since the question's option said the draft also survives closing the tab). Asking on close with no draft, and saving each note as it is made, were the other options.
     5. **The editor is fitted onto the tab's own pages first.** The slice's first task tries pdf.js's editor layer on the page view 6q-5 built (find, links, the reading place and thumbnails stay as they are), as a go or no-go gate. If it fails, the user gets a new plan that moves the tab to pdf.js's `PDFViewer`. Moving to `PDFViewer` now was the other option.
     6. **A file changed on disk never takes unsaved notes along.** A bar says the file changed and the notes aren't saved, and offers Save as a copy, Reload (which drops the notes) and Overwrite. Carrying the notes onto the new file, and offering only Save as a copy, were the other options.
     7. **Notes other apps made can be changed.** Any highlight or free text in the file, from Acrobat or Edge too, can be recoloured, commented, moved or deleted, with the same save, backup and Undo. Other kinds (stamps, ink, sticky notes) show as they are and are listed in Notes, read-only. Showing other apps' notes only was the other option.
     8. **Builders are as in 6q-5.** The main thread writes the editor's fit and the save path; Sonnet workers take the Notes view, the bars and the mechanical tasks, each checked in the main thread. Codex CLI, and no workers, were the other options.
     9. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **pdf.js's editor listens only while the keyboard is in the tab.** Its mode is NONE whenever the keyboard is outside the PDF tab or the window is away, so Ctrl+Z, Ctrl+A, Backspace, Delete, Enter, Space and Esc always reach the terminal (research §2.6, the key hijack). The person's tool stays chosen and comes back with the keyboard.
        - **Esc on the pages ends a note being typed or unselects a note first,** and only then goes to the terminal.
        - **A draft is the whole PDF with its notes,** kept by the core in `<home>/drafts/pdf/`, put 1.5 s after the last change. PDF drafts take at most 1 GB in all; past that, the tab keeps its notes and says "Marshell keeps at most 1 GB of unsaved PDF notes. Save some PDFs first." A save of a PDF may be up to 58 MB, the size a PDF tab opens, so a PDF saved with its notes always opens again (the user's choice in the PR review, 2026-11-20; a tab opened 50 MB before); other saves stay at 2 MB.
        - **An undone save keeps the notes,** unsaved again, as an undone text save keeps the edits (6q-4 decision 8).
        - **Save as a copy writes `{name} (notes).pdf` beside the file** (then `(notes 2)` and up) and opens it in a new tab. There is no Save As dialog.
        - **Notes are off, with the reason in the tools' tooltip,** for a PDF that is signed, an XFA form, read-only on disk, or encrypted without the owner's permission for notes. An encrypted PDF that allows notes takes them if the first task finds pdf.js writes them so Edge can read them; otherwise it is off too.
        - **The selection bar is Marshell's own,** under the end of the selection, and never shows while the Highlight tool is on (the tool highlights at once). Free-hand (drawn) highlights stay out, with ink.
        - **The new colour is the one last picked** while Marshell runs; free text is pdf.js's 10 pt black, with no size or colour setting.
        - **The sidebar button becomes "Outline, pages and notes",** the palette gains "Show PDF notes", and Save and Undo save list for a PDF tab. Under 360 px the zoom percentage hides, as the word "Page" does, so the two tools fit a 320 px pane.
     10. **Settled while building, and what the live check found** (`docs/research/2026-11-19-pdf-notes-live-check.md`; ten findings, each fixed with a test that failed first).
        - **pdf.js's editor stays in its mode, and Marshell decides which keys it sees.** Switching pdf.js to NONE when the keyboard leaves redraws every page and rebuilds the file's notes, so 9's first line is kept by a key gate instead: outside the tab pdf.js sees no key; on the pages it sees Ctrl+Z and Ctrl+Y, and Enter and Space only with Free text on. With no tool on, a note can still be selected, moved and deleted. Notes are never copied, cut, pasted or dropped, because pdf.js would take every copy and paste in the window, and a press on blank page draws nothing (no free-hand highlights).
        - **An encrypted PDF takes notes when its owner allows them.** pdf.js writes them into an RC4-128 file so that Edge reads them, and into an AES-256 file so that pypdf decrypts them. A file whose owner forbids notes says "This PDF's owner doesn't allow notes."
        - **A PDF counts as signed when pdf.js's list of signatures has one:** a signature value with a real byte range around its contents. pdf.js gives every signature field the value null, so the field's value can't tell. A placeholder signature (`/ByteRange [0 0 0 0]`) is not a signature and keeps the tools.
        - **The comment popup is a dialog that keeps the keyboard until it ends:** Tab goes round inside it, and Done, Cancel, Esc, Ctrl+Enter or a press outside end it. Done with the text emptied deletes the note's comment. A popup whose page is dropped (scrolled far away) ends as Cancel. The popup, the selection bar and its colour menu follow the app's theme, though the page forces a light one.
        - **The announcements come from the notes themselves,** compared before and after each change, because pdf.js also reports the file's own notes as added. An undo that removes a note says "Note deleted.", and its redo says the note again.
        - **The Notes view names a colour only when it is one of the five;** an Acrobat yellow reads "Highlight" with its swatch. A highlight's text is the whole words whose middle lies under it. A pick puts the keyboard on the note only when the sidebar lies over the pages; beside them the keyboard stays in the list, as in the outline.
        - **Find's Highlight keeps the keyboard on its button,** so a run of Next and Highlight goes on. Find's Comment moves it to the popup.
        - **A page drawn again (a zoom, a scroll back, a tab shown again) adds no undo step** and leaves a saved tab saved.
        - **A turned page's text and notes are turned with it,** by pdf.js's own layout rules in the stylesheet block adapted from pdf.js. Before, on a page with `/Rotate`, a selection, find's matches and every note landed where the page would be unturned, and saved notes were misplaced in other readers too.
        - **A drag let go past a line's end selects up to that line.** Each text layer ends with pdf.js's end-of-content block, which covers the layer while a press made in it is held. Before, the selection ran on to the page's last lines, and a highlight made from it covered them all.
        - **A draft that failed to be taken is taken again** when the tab goes or the window loses focus, and a hidden tab with no page drawn takes its draft like any other. Before, a tab left within 1.5 s of a note lost the note once its panel was unloaded.
        - **A PDF that is gone when its tab opens, and has a draft, opens on the draft** under "{name} was deleted. Your notes aren't saved.", with Save as a copy and Save. This holds across a quit and start.
        - **A bar's buttons (Reload, Overwrite, the deleted bar's Save, the save line's Undo) put the keyboard on the pages before they act,** because the bar goes with the button.
        - **Settled by the final review.** Other apps' stamps stay drawn on the page and never go to pdf.js's editor, which needs a structure tree and the stamp's own canvas. Other apps' ink is drawn by pdf.js's editor but takes no pointer or keyboard, so it stays read-only. A read-only file gets no editor while its notes are all saved. One that turns read-only with unsaved notes keeps them on screen, and Ctrl+S there says "{name} is read-only, so the notes can't be saved into it." and offers Save as a copy. The dot and the close question count a save in flight as unsaved, and a tab that goes during a save waits for the answer, keeping its notes in a draft when they didn't reach the file. The switched-off tools are `aria-disabled`, so the keyboard reaches their reason. pdf.js sees only the keys aimed inside its own tab.
        - **Settled by the PR review (2026-11-20).** A change made while a save is under way stays unsaved after it and goes on to a draft, and a tab that goes during a save, or while a note is still being typed, keeps those notes in a draft. A window losing focus still leaves the typing alone. A PDF tab opens up to 58 MB, the size a save may write (the user's choice). The Notes view, chosen last, opens again on a PDF with no outline.
        - **Lines the plan didn't have:** "{name} is over 58 MB, so it can't be saved.", "The notes couldn't be written into {name}.", "{stem} (notes).pdf to {stem} (notes 99).pdf are all taken, so no copy was saved.", "The save of {name} couldn't be undone. {line}" and "{name} is read-only, so the notes can't be saved into it."
   - **Resize-polish slice decisions (2026-11-20).** What 6q-0 decision 6 left for a slice after 6q (`docs/spikes/S12-browser-pane.md`, findings 22 and 23), with the keyboard leads the 6q-2 live check left open. Asked of the user on 2026-11-20, when the slice was planned. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-20-resize-polish.md`.
     1. **The shell panel is as tall as whole terminal rows, in the frame the window changes.** It keeps its share (30% of the sidebar, within 120 to 280 px), cut down to the rows that fit, so no part of a row shows under the last line and the panel changes one row at a time. Until its terminal has measured a row, the panel is as tall as before. A height the user drags and that is saved, and leaving the height alone, were the other options. **Reversed after the hand check (2026-11-20):** on a drag of the window's edge the user found the row-at-a-time steps worse than the smooth resize, which they had called fine before the change, so the panel is 30% of the sidebar by the pixel again and up to 16 px of a part row can show under the last line. The whole-rows code is out; decision 4's same-frame measure stays (`docs/research/2026-11-20-resize-polish-live-check.md`).
     2. **The window buttons get a spike first.** WebView2 draws a resized webview a frame or more late (S12 finding 23), so buttons drawn in the webview trail on left- and right-edge drags, and nothing inside the webview can change that. The slice's first task tries the three buttons in a small native child of fixed size that Windows moves with the frame, as a go or no-go gate judged by the user's eye. On go the strip is planned and built next. On no-go the trailing is recorded as a limit and the buttons stay. Recording the limit now, and the system's own title bar, were the other options. **The spike ended go (2026-11-20, `docs/spikes/S13-window-buttons-strip.md`):** a plain Win32 child window kept up with the edge on every drag and left the keyboard where it was; a child webview trailed like the page and took the keyboard. Asked the same day, the user chose to build the strip as its own slice straight after this one, with its own plan and questions, over adding it to this slice or leaving it in the backlog. Until then the buttons stay as they are.
     3. **Leaving a pane that covers the terminal hides the pane.** With under 720 px of room the pane covers the terminal. Esc and Ctrl+Shift+Tab in a pane tab then hide the pane, with its usual motion, and the terminal takes the keyboard. Moving the keyboard to the session list, and leaving it in the tab with a line saying why, were the other options.
     4. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **The window's size reaches the layout in the frame it changes.** The app measured its window with an observer and drew from the measure a frame later, so everything it computes from the size (the shell panel's height, the pane's width at its limits, the rail) trailed the window by a frame. The measure is now applied before that frame is drawn.
        - **The hide is announced:** "The side pane is hidden. Ctrl+Shift+Q shows it." Only this hide speaks, because the key that caused it doesn't name the pane. Ctrl+Shift+Tab has the cursor's receipt as well, and the announcer holds one notice, so there the two are one line: "Selected session 2, api. The side pane is hidden. Ctrl+Shift+Q shows it." (found by the live check: said apart, the receipt replaced the hide before it was drawn).
        - **A focused divider keeps the keyboard through a layout motion,** and its keys do nothing until the motion ends. When the divider goes because the window got so narrow that the pane covers the terminal, the keyboard moves into the pane.
        - **xterm is told it runs on ConPTY only if a measured run is better for it.** The live check prints numbered lines in a shell, shrinks and grows the rows, and counts lost, doubled and blank lines with xterm's `windowsPty` option off and on. The option ships only if the count is lower with it on. Windows 11 or Windows 10 is read from the webview (`platformVersion`), which is the one line xterm draws. **Measured 2026-11-20: not better, so the option stays unset.** Both runs lost, doubled and blanked nothing over 80 resizes, and with the option on the prompt ended ten rows above the bottom (`docs/research/2026-11-20-resize-polish-live-check.md`).
        - **Limits this slice records and doesn't change.** The sidebar's bottom bar shifts for a frame on bottom-edge drags, and the address bar's buttons trail on left- and right-edge drags, both by WebView2's late frame. Left for later: the history player's and the scripted terminal's own fits, a page's scrollbar jumping while the divider moves (S12 finding 23's untested ideas), and the other paths that can leave the keyboard in a covered terminal (an overlay that closes while the pane covers).
   - **Soft surfaces decisions (2026-11-20).** Asked of the user on 2026-11-20, as a change of its own beside the resize-polish slice.
     1. **Dark mode is a dark grey, not true black.** The window is `oklch(18% 0 none)`, which draws as #121212 (the option was shown as "about #131313"), with the raised and overlay surfaces a step lighter each. A lighter grey (about #1e1e1e) and a barely-off-black (about #0c0c0c) were the other options.
     2. **Light mode is a soft warm white, not pure white.** The window is about #f6f5f3 (`oklch(97% 0.003 85)`), with the raised and overlay surfaces a step darker each. A dimmer warm grey (about #eeede9) and a barely-off-white (about #fbfaf8) were the other options.
     3. **High contrast keeps true black and pure white.** With High contrast on, by the setting or by Windows asking for more contrast, the surfaces are the ones both themes had before this change.
     4. **Marshell's default terminal theme follows the window** in all four cases. An imported terminal theme keeps its own colours. Leaving the terminal black and white was the other option.
     5. **The window's own theme follows the Appearance theme** (added 2026-11-20, after the user saw a dark right-click menu on the light app). WebView2 draws its right-click menu in the theme of the native window, which was the system's whatever Appearance said. The app now sends its theme to the window (`set_window_theme`: light, dark, or the system's when Appearance follows the system), and the window passes it to its webviews. The user allowed the one new capability line, `allow-set-window-theme`, which like the others names only the app's own webview. Giving `set_window_background` a second argument, and leaving the menu, were the other options.
   - **Window-buttons strip slice decisions (2026-11-20).** The strip that resize-polish decision 2 left for a slice of its own, after spike S13 ended go (`docs/spikes/S13-window-buttons-strip.md`). Asked of the user on 2026-11-20, when the slice was planned. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-20-window-buttons-strip.md`. Windows only: on macOS and Linux nothing changes.
     1. **The strip holds the three window buttons only:** Minimize, Maximize or Restore, and Close. The side-pane toggle beside them stays in the page, with its tooltip, its "new tab" dot and its disabled state, and trails on a right-edge drag like the rest of the bar. The user judges that in the hand check; adding the toggle to the strip was the other option and can follow.
     2. **Both snap leads join the slice, each as a gated task** that is dropped and recorded if it doesn't work. Hovering Maximize shows Windows 11's Snap Layouts (issue #23): the strip answers the hit test over Maximize as the system's maximize button. And Win+Left and Win+Right, which don't snap the window today (S13 finding 5): a task finds the cause and fixes it if the fix is in the app's own window code. Taking neither was the other option.
     3. **One new command, `set_window_strip`, with its capability line `allow-set-window-strip`,** which like the others names only the app's own webview. The user allowed the line. The page uses it to give the strip its pictures and to say when it shows. Passing the strip's state as extra arguments of `set_window_background` was the other option.
     4. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
        - **The page paints the strip's pictures.** For each button the page draws two small pictures on a canvas, at rest and under the pointer, from the colours its own buttons have at that moment, and sends them to the strip, which only shows them. So the theme, High contrast, forced colours and the display scale reach the strip without a second set of colours in native code, and the glyphs are drawn by the same engine that draws the page's. The pictures are sent again when any of those change.
        - **The page's buttons stay** where they are, under the strip: they are what the keyboard and a screen reader use, and what shows on macOS and Linux, before the first pictures arrive and whenever the strip is hidden. The strip is hidden while anything is drawn over the page (a dialog, a menu, a drag: whatever calls `useCoverPage`), and while keyboard focus shows on one of the three buttons, so the page's focus ring is seen.
        - **A click on the strip does what the page's button does,** through the same window calls. Close therefore still asks about the tray the first time. The strip never takes the keyboard: it stays where it was, and a press on a Marshell window that isn't in front brings the window to the front first.
        - **A pressed button looks like a hovered one,** as the page's buttons do today (the hover rule comes after the pressed rule in the stylesheet).
        - **The page's three buttons fill the bar's height above its hairline** (39 px of the 40, where they were 40 px centred on 39), so the page and the strip draw the same rows.
        - **The strip leaves the window's top edge alone:** the top edge resizes the window on the row above the page, which the strip doesn't cover, and the strip's buttons take the pointer over their whole height, as the page's buttons do. (First written as a band at the strip's top that lets the pointer through; the first look found no window below it to take the point. Tauri makes its top-edge resize window only when the app's webview is the window's content, and under the `unstable` feature it is a child webview.)
        - **The strip's window is named "Window buttons"** for a screen reader that follows the mouse. It says nothing more: the page's buttons are the ones with names and roles.
        - **Limits this slice records and doesn't change.** The side-pane toggle and the address bar's buttons still trail on left- and right-edge drags.
     5. **What the live check settled** (`docs/research/2026-11-20-window-buttons-strip-live-check.md`). Decided while building, not asked; the user can change any of them.
        - **Both gates ended go.** Hovering Maximize shows Snap Layouts and a picked layout snaps the window, so issue #23 closes. Win+Left and Win+Right already snap the window, so that lead needed no change.
        - **The webview's window clips its siblings.** wry makes the window the page lives in without the `WS_CLIPSIBLINGS` style, and such a window paints over a sibling above it: the strip took the pointer and never showed. The strip's code sets the style on every webview window of the main window, a browser page's included, when the strip is made and whenever it shows. Nothing in `tauri.conf.json`, `tauri.windows.conf.json` or the capabilities changed for it.
        - **Onboarding counts as covering the page,** so the strip hides under it like under any dialog.
        - **The performance panel starts below the bar,** where the strip can't lie over it.
        - **The strip's colours come from three hidden probe buttons** (rest, hover, Close's hover), never from a button the pointer can be on.
        - **After the strip moves, a pointer that is no longer on it lights nothing.** A click on Maximize or Restore moves the strip away from a pointer that stays put.
   - **Slice 6r decisions (2026-11-20).** The side pane becomes separate panels: Browser, Files and Changes, each with its own button in the title bar, shown alone or stacked. Asked of the user on 2026-11-20, from their screenshots of another app's panels (the mechanics are the model; the look is Marshell's own). The slices are 6r-1a, 6r-1b, 6r-2, 6r-3 and 6r-4, each with its own plan, questions, live check and pull request. This overturns "There is no file tree; that stays in v2" (slice 2.5's Preview scope): 6r-2 builds one.
     1. **The order.** 6r-1: the frame (the buttons, the stacked panels, today's tabs split between Browser and Files, no tree yet) with the Browser panel's menu. 6r-2: the file tree, the path bar, "Collapse all" and the Files menu. 6r-3: Changes (the local git diff, with line comments handed to the agent). 6r-4: GitHub sync. "Slice 6r-1a decisions" 1 cut 6r-1 in two.
     2. **The series runs after slice 6s and before 6n3 and 7d.**
     3. **Which panels are open, and their sizes, belong to the window. What a panel holds belongs to the session on screen:** its files, its pages, the tree's state and the diff. Moving between sessions swaps the content and never the panels (as 6q-2 decision 4 has it for the pane).
     4. **Changes compares the merge base with main to the working tree,** so it shows the branch's commits together with uncommitted and new files.
     5. **GitHub (6r-4):** the pull request's and CI's status, "Create pull request" with its push, merge (or auto-merge where the repository allows it), and the review comments on their diff lines, each of which can be handed to the agent. It needs `gh`.
     6. **The one "Side pane" button goes, and one button per panel takes its place.** Ctrl+Shift+Q still hides and shows the whole pane, and brings back the panels that were open.
     7. **The panels stack in a fixed order, top to bottom: Browser, Files, Changes.** The dividers between them move by dragging and by keyboard. A panel's ⤢ button fills the pane with that panel.
     8. **Browser holds web pages and HTML files. Files holds every other file:** text and code, CSV, PDF, images and Markdown.
     9. **The Changes button arrives with its panel in 6r-3.** 6r-1 adds the Browser and Files buttons only.
     10. **Each panel has a show/hide command with a default key,** which the plan picks from the free ones and which is rebindable like every command once rebinding is built (§7's "all bindings are rebindable").
     11. **The Browser panel's ⋯ menu:** Copy link; Save screenshot; Open HTML file…; Open in the system browser (it moves there from the address bar); Clear browsing data; and four settings: Ctrl+click on a web link in terminal output opens it in the Browser panel; an HTML file an agent writes opens by itself (for every CLI that reports file writes; the others say they can't); viewport sizes (responsive, or a fixed phone, tablet or desktop width); and keeping cookies (until quit, always, never). The reference's "Auto-verify changes" and "Manage allowed sites…" are left out: Marshell's agents don't drive the page, they have only `marshell open` and region to prompt.
     12. **The Files panel's ⋯ menu (6r-2):** Go to file or search (today's "Open file…" quick open); Show file tree; File tree position (Automatic, Left, Right); Collapse all folders; Preview tabs (a single click opens a temporary tab, its name in italics); Auto save (after a pause and on leaving the tab, the same safe save as Ctrl+S, stopping at a changed-on-disk conflict); Word wrap; Tab key indents (on by default; off makes Tab move focus); Indent guides; Show whitespace; Scroll past end; Tab size (2, 4, 8). One set of editor settings for all files, saved with the app's settings. The reference's Ctrl+Shift+Y and Ctrl+P aren't used: the first is History, and the second is the shell's previous-command key.
     13. **The Files panel's look (6r-2):** a filter box at the top of the tree ("Filter, or ? to search in files"), the open file marked in the tree, an icon per file type, long names shortened in the middle, and a path bar that shows the path from the project folder. Whether "?" searches file contents is asked when 6r-2 is planned.
   - **Slice 6r-1a decisions (2026-11-21).** The frame: the Browser and Files panels, their title-bar buttons and keys, the stack and its divider, and the part of the Browser menu that needs nothing new. Asked of the user on 2026-11-21, when the slice was planned, after a read of the pane's, the page's and the keymap's code (1 to 9); 10 is what the plan settled without asking. The user took the recommended option each time; for 5 they gave a note in place of a pick ("make sure to make it nice looking in terms of ux/ui with a icon and intuative looking buttons") and then chose its contents. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-21-phase-6r-1a-side-pane-panels.md`.
     1. **6r-1 runs as two slices.** 6r-1a builds the frame and a ⋯ menu with what already exists: Copy link, Open in the system browser, Open HTML file… and Clear browsing data. 6r-1b builds the five new things of "Slice 6r decisions" 11: Save screenshot, the viewport sizes, keeping cookies, the Ctrl+click setting and the auto-open of HTML files an agent writes. No CLI reports a file write to the app today, so the last one needs new plumbing per CLI. One slice, and a frame with no menu, were the other options.
     2. **An HTML file shows in the Browser panel in today's viewer:** no scripts run, and the Rendered and Source toggle stays. 6r-1b spikes showing a local HTML file as a real page, where its scripts run, and brings the user the security question with the facts: a page may load only `http`, `https` and `about:blank` today (6q-3 decision 12). A real page in this slice, and the viewer for good, were the other options.
     3. **An open into a hidden panel adds that panel to the stack.** Only Browser is shown and a file path is Ctrl+clicked: Files shows too, under Browser, and takes the keyboard. Nothing on screen leaves; the person hides a panel when they want the room. It ends a fill (10). Swapping to the target panel, and keeping a fill by swapping the filled panel, were the other options.
     4. **Each panel is a stop for Ctrl+Shift+Tab:** terminal, Browser, Files, terminal, with hidden panels skipped. Ctrl+Tab steps the tabs of the panel that has the keyboard, or had it last when the keyboard is in the terminal. Going between the terminal and the last panel only, and one ring of every tab across both panels, were the other options.
     5. **An empty Browser panel shows a centred block:** a globe icon, the heading "No pages open", the line "Open an address, an HTML file or a server this session runs.", a filled "New tab" button with a plus icon and a quieter "Open HTML file…" button with a file icon. Under them, "Running in this session" lists each port the session listens on as a button (`localhost:3000`) that opens it in a tab; the row is left out when the session listens on nothing, and the line then reads "Open an address or an HTML file." Closing the last tab leaves this block, and the panel stays shown. A blank tab that is always there, and the block without the ports, were the other options.
     6. **The empty Files panel gets the same look:** a folder icon, "No files open", "Open a file from this project, or drop one here.", a filled "Open file…" button with a search icon and a quieter "Browse…" button, then "Recent in this project" with its list. The separate "Drop a file here to open it." line goes into the help line. Leaving it as it was until 6r-2 was the other option.
     7. **The keys:** Ctrl+Shift+A shows and hides Browser, Ctrl+Shift+D Files, and Ctrl+Shift+H is kept for Changes (6r-3). All three are free on Windows and Linux. On macOS the app's modifier is ⌘ alone, where ⌘A is Select all and ⌘H is Hide: Browser takes ⌘⌥A there (as the side pane takes ⌘⌥Q), Files takes ⌘D, and the macOS key for Changes is picked in 6r-3. Ctrl+Shift+H with Ctrl+Shift+E (taken on Linux desktops), and Ctrl+Shift+P with Ctrl+Shift+D (Ctrl+Shift+P is another editor's palette), were the other options.
     8. **A panel's key and its title-bar button are plain toggles:** a shown panel hides, wherever the keyboard is, as Ctrl+Shift+Q does for the pane. Ctrl+Shift+Tab is the way into a shown panel. A key that focuses a shown panel first and hides it on the second press was the other option.
     9. **With nothing remembered, Ctrl+Shift+Q shows the panels that hold tabs:** Browser when the session on screen has web or HTML tabs, Files when it has other files, both when it has both, and Files alone when it has none. That is the case on a first run and at the first start after the update, where a pane that was open shows by the same rule, so nobody's open tabs go missing. Files alone, and both panels always, were the other options.
     10. **Decided, not asked.** The plan settles these; the user can change any of them.
         - **A tab's panel follows what it shows.** A browser tab, and a file tab whose file ends in `.html` or `.htm`, are in Browser; every other file tab is in Files. A link in a file tab that leads to a file of the other panel opens there as a tab of its own, so a tab doesn't change panel. A tab saved by an older build with both kinds in its history moves when Back or Forward crosses them, and the keyboard goes with it when it was in the tab. With 32 tabs open such a link is refused, as any open is. The Files panel's recent list still names HTML files; one opens in Browser.
         - **Where browser pages aren't available** (macOS and Linux for now, 6q-3 decision 12), the Browser panel still shows HTML files, and its "New tab" is disabled with "Not available on this system yet". Its empty block then leads with "Open HTML file…", reads "Open an HTML file. Web pages aren't available on this system yet." and lists no ports, and the menu's "Clear browsing data…" is disabled with the same reason.
         - **A panel's bar** holds its tabs, then +, then for Browser the ⋯ menu ("Browser menu"), then ⤢ and ×. Browser's + ("New tab") makes a blank tab with the keyboard in its Address box. Files' + ("Open a file") opens the menu it had: Open file…, Browse… and the recent files, without "New browser tab". × reads "Hide Browser (Ctrl+Shift+A)" or "Hide Files (Ctrl+Shift+D)". The pane's own "Hide the side pane" button goes.
         - **The title-bar buttons** sit where the Side pane button was, Browser then Files, a globe and a folder. Each has `aria-expanded` and reads "Show Browser (Ctrl+Shift+A)" or "Hide Browser (Ctrl+Shift+A)". With no session, or while a view replaces the terminal, both are `aria-disabled` and read "The side pane shows beside a session". A panel's button carries the dot, and " · A new tab is waiting", for a tab `marshell open` added to that panel. The navigation cursor reaches both, named "Browser" and "Files", and Ctrl+Shift+Tab says "Selected Browser" or "Selected Files". Its command reads "Go to the next terminal or panel".
         - **Ctrl+Shift+Q's memory.** Hiding the pane keeps the panels that were shown. Hiding the last shown panel with its own key or × hides the pane and keeps that panel as the one Ctrl+Shift+Q brings back. While the pane is hidden, whatever shows a panel (its key, its button, an open) shows that panel alone.
         - **The stack's divider** reads "Resize Browser and Files". It moves by dragging, by Up and Down (16 px), by Home and End, and a double-click makes the two equal. A panel is at least 160 px tall; in a pane too short for that the two are equal. The heights are saved as weights, not pixels, so they hold when the window's height changes.
         - **⤢ fills the pane with its panel** and hides the others until it is pressed again (named "Fill the side pane", with `aria-pressed`; its tooltip reads "Show the other panels" while it fills). Showing or opening into another panel ends the fill, and hiding the filled panel brings the others back. With one panel shown the button is `aria-disabled` and reads "No other panel is shown". The fill is saved with the layout.
         - **A panel that shows, hides, fills or is restored moves for 200 ms on the app's curve,** as every layout change does (6q-2 decision 6), and at once with reduced motion. A page in the Browser panel follows its panel frame by frame while the height moves, as it does when the sidebar folds beside it; it is still a picture while the whole pane folds (6q-3 decision 14), and the panels inside keep their heights through that fold. While the window's height is dragged the page keeps to its panel: its placement rule carries the share of each added pixel that goes to the panels below it. Dragging the divider is never animated.
         - **The keyboard.** A panel that shows takes the keyboard. A panel that hides gives it to the terminal when it had it, and otherwise leaves it where it is; the hide is announced ("Browser is hidden."). In a pane that covers the terminal, a panel that hides with the keyboard gives it to the panel that stays, since the terminal is out of sight. The commands that act on the tab on screen (Next tab, Close tab, Select region, the editor's and the PDF's) take the panel that has the keyboard, or had it last when that panel is on screen and holds tabs, else the first panel on screen that holds tabs; Select region and the editor's and PDF's commands fall to the other panel on screen when that panel's tab can't take them, and "No tabs open in Browser." is said for a panel with none. In a pane that covers the terminal (resize-polish decision 3), Esc in a tab still hides the pane, and Ctrl+Shift+Tab hides it from the last shown panel, where the terminal is the next stop.
         - **The words.** The whole stays "the side pane" (the command, the divider beside the terminal, the drop label, the Ctrl+click hints, "The side pane is hidden. Ctrl+Shift+Q shows it."). An open names its panel: "Opened README.md in Files.", "Opened localhost:3000 in Browser.", "Claude Code opened report.html in Browser." A file dropped on the pane opens in the panel its kind belongs to, wherever on the pane it was dropped. The palette gains "Browser panel" and "Files panel".
         - **Tabs.** A session still holds at most 32 tabs, counted over both panels. Each panel has its own selected tab and keeps its own four most recent tabs loaded (6q-2 decision 13); the eight loaded pages are still counted over the whole app (6q-3 decision 4). `marshell open` selects its tab only when that panel of the session had none, and still never shows a panel. A window a page opens while the Browser panel isn't on screen becomes a tab the same quiet way, with the dot on Browser's button; until now it showed the pane.
         - **The ⋯ menu's four rows.** "Copy link" copies the address of the page on screen. "Open in the system browser" opens it there, and the button leaves the address bar. Both are disabled, with their reason, when the selected tab is blank, an HTML file, or there is none ("No page is open", "An HTML file has no address"). "Open HTML file…" is the system's file dialog. "Clear browsing data…" opens the question the palette's "Clear the browser's data…" opens.
         - **`layout.json`.** `view.pane` gains the panels that are shown, their height weights and the filled panel, and each session the Browser panel's selected tab beside the one it had, which stays the Files panel's (and names the Browser panel's tab while Files has none, so a session with tabs always names one). A new field that can't be read costs only itself. The version stays 1: an old file loads with nothing remembered (9), and an older build ignores the new fields and shows its one pane.
         - **Sonnet workers implement the plan's larger tasks,** each checked in the main thread.
   - **Slice 6r-1b decisions (2026-11-21).** The Browser menu's five new things ("Slice 6r decisions" 11), the spike of a local HTML file as a real page, and what slice 6r-1a's reviews left (issue #161). 1 to 4 are the user's answers of 2026-11-21 to 6r-1a's open questions, recorded on issue #161 and built first. 5 to 12 were asked on 2026-11-21, when the slice was planned, after a read of the page's, the menu's, the settings' and each CLI's hook code, and 13 to 27 the same day after spike S14 (`docs/spikes/S14-local-html-page.md`); the user took the recommended option each time but one (15, where they chose four sizes). 28 is what the plan settled without asking, and 29 was asked the same day after the plan's review. The plan is `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-11-21-phase-6r-1b-browser-menu.md`.
     1. **The cursor speaks real names for every title-bar target:** "Cursor on Browser", "Cursor on Files", "Cursor on Command palette", "Cursor on Sidebar", not their ids.
     2. **A panel's Hide button shows a short horizontal bar, not an ×,** so it can't be read as a tab's close button or as "close all tabs". Its name and tooltip stay "Hide Browser (Ctrl+Shift+A)" and "Hide Files (Ctrl+Shift+D)".
     3. **Each Fill button names its panel:** "Fill the side pane with Browser" and "Fill the side pane with Files". The tooltip while a panel fills stays "Show the other panels". This replaces "Fill the side pane" in "Slice 6r-1a decisions" 10.
     4. **A popup that can't open at the 32-tab limit says the limit line,** "This session has 32 tabs. Close one to open another.", the line a drop gets. Kept as built, by the same answers: a short empty panel scrolls inside itself; a palette command the current panel's tab can't take acts on the other panel's tab when that one can; "Close tab" behind a hidden pane closes nothing and says "The side pane is hidden. Ctrl+Shift+Q shows it."
     5. **The spike runs first, and a real page is built later.** Before the plan is written, a spike in the spikes workspace shows a local HTML file as a real page and measures what its scripts can reach; it also checks the WebView2 facts the viewport, cookie and full-page screenshot questions need. The user then gets the security question with those facts. An approved real page is built as its own slice, 6r-1c. Building it inside 6r-1b, and running the spike as the slice's last task, were the other options.
     6. **"Save screenshot" saves to the Downloads folder without asking,** where a page's downloads go (6q-3 decision 5), under a name like "example.com 2026-11-21 134501.png", and the announcer says "Saved example.com 2026-11-21 134501.png to Downloads." Pictures\Screenshots, the system's Save dialog each time, and the session's project folder were the other options. "Select region" stays the way to hand a picture to the agent.
     7. **An HTML file an agent writes opens quietly, as `marshell open` does.** The tab is added to the agent's session without being selected (it is selected only when Browser held no tab), and the Browser button gets the dot and "A new tab is waiting". A hidden panel stays hidden, the terminal doesn't resize and the keyboard never moves. Showing the panel in the session on screen, and selecting the tab only when Browser is already shown, were the other options.
     8. **It acts on a file the agent creates or edits.** A file that isn't open gets a tab. A file already open reloads in place as it does today, and its tab gets the dot unless it is the tab in front of the person, as with `marshell open` on an open file. Created files only, and no dot on open tabs, were the other options.
     9. **The four settings sit in the ⋯ menu itself.** The menu reads: Copy link, Save screenshot, Open in the system browser; Open HTML file…; "Open terminal links here" and "Open HTML files agents write" as checked items, then "Viewport" and "Cookies", each a submenu of radio items; Clear browsing data…. One "Settings" submenu, and a single item that leads to the Settings page, were the other options.
     10. **The Settings page gains a "Browser" section with the same settings,** each with a line that explains it. The menu and the page change the same saved values. The menu alone was the other option.
     11. **"Open HTML files agents write" is on for someone who never touched it.** It adds only a background tab and a dot. It needs the CLI's hooks; a session without them says it can't. Off was the other option.
     12. **Every HTML file written gets its tab, up to the session's 32.** The first refusal is announced once ("This session has 32 tabs. Close one to open another."), and the rest are skipped without a word until a tab closes. At most three per prompt, and one tab that the newest file replaces, were the other options.
     13. **A local HTML file may run as a real page, one file at a time (6r-1c).** Asked after spike S14 (`docs/spikes/S14-local-html-page.md`), with its facts: served from its folder under a host name of its own, a page's scripts and modules run and can read every file under that folder and send it to the internet; they can't read a file outside it, Marshell's API or its token, or call the app. An HTML file keeps opening in today's viewer with scripts off, and a "Run scripts" button in that tab turns it into a real page after one line that says what the page can then read, so nothing an agent writes or a cloned repository holds runs by itself. `file://` stays refused: there a page can pull in any script or image on the disk. Which folder is served and how the choice is remembered are asked when 6r-1c is planned. A setting that is off, every HTML file as a real page, and the viewer for good were the other options.
     14. **A viewport size belongs to the tab.** The Viewport submenu changes the tab in front and is saved with it; a new tab starts Responsive. The session, and the whole app, were the other options. The Settings page's Browser section (10) therefore holds the other three settings.
     15. **The sizes are Responsive, "Phone (390 px)", "Tablet (768 px)", "Laptop (1024 px)" and "Desktop (1440 px)".** Width only: the height follows the panel, and no touch or phone user agent is faked. A page narrower than its panel is centred with empty margins; a wider one is drawn scaled down to fit (S14). Three sizes with a 1280 or a 1440 desktop were the other options.
     16. **A fixed viewport shows as a chip at the end of the address bar,** "Phone 390" or "Desktop 1440 · 25%" with the scale when the page is scaled, and activating it opens the same Viewport choices. Responsive shows no chip. The menu's radio mark alone was the other option.
     17. **"Never" for cookies means private pages.** WebView2 can't refuse cookies outright (S14). The three choices: "Always" keeps them on disk, as until now; "Until quit" clears cookies and site storage when Marshell quits and at its next start; "Never" makes every Browser tab a private page, whose cookies and site storage live in memory only and are gone once the last Browser tab closes or Marshell quits. Clearing a site's data when its last tab closes, and dropping "Never", were the other options.
     18. **"Always" is the cookie rule for someone who never touched it,** so nothing changes at the update. "Until quit" was the other option.
     19. **Two screenshot rows:** "Save screenshot" saves the part of the page on screen, and "Save full-page screenshot" the page from top to bottom, both as 6 has it. With a fixed viewport the picture has that viewport's width at full size. This adds a row to the menu of 9. The part on screen only, and the whole page only, were the other options.
     20. **The palette gains the screenshots and the viewport:** "Save screenshot", "Save full-page screenshot", and "Viewport: Responsive", "Viewport: Phone", "Viewport: Tablet", "Viewport: Laptop" and "Viewport: Desktop", with no default key, for the Browser tab in front. The two on/off settings and Cookies stay in the menu and in Settings. The screenshots only, and none, were the other options.
     21. **Two shell commands are added, with their permission lines** (the user's word on the security surface, as `SCOPE.md` asks): `browser_screenshot`, which asks the page on screen for its picture, and `browser_cookies`, which tells the shell the cookie rule. Each gets one line in `src-tauri/capabilities/default.json`, for the app's own webview only, and does one fixed thing: no command takes a DevTools method name or a size from the app (S14: an oversized capture took a page down). The shell starts using `serde_json`, already built into the app, to read a page's measured size. The viewport needs no command: it rides on the placement report. One more argument on today's commands, and leaving both features out, were the other options.
     22. **Switching the cookie rule to or from "Never" loads every loaded Browser tab again,** in every session, since a private page is decided when it is made. It is announced: "Cookies: Never. 3 tabs were loaded again." Between "Always" and "Until quit" nothing is loaded again. Only tabs loaded from then on, and after a restart, were the other options.
     23. **"Save full-page screenshot" on a page taller than 16,384 pixels saves the top 16,384 and says so:** "Saved the first 16,384 pixels of the page as example.com 2026-11-21 134501.png to Downloads." One picture is wrong below that height (S14). Several files, and a refusal, were the other options.
     24. **The viewport sizes work for an HTML file tab too:** the viewer's frame takes the same widths, centred or drawn scaled down, with the same chip. The two screenshot rows are off for an HTML file, with the reason shown, since the viewer has no page to capture. Web pages only was the other option.
     25. **A session that can't open an agent's HTML files says so in the menu and in Settings.** The menu row stays a working switch, and for the session on screen it carries a line: "Not in this session: Codex's hooks aren't set up." Settings' Browser section lists each CLI found with "Opens its HTML files" or the reason, and says that a file written by a shell command isn't seen. A plain shell has nothing to say. Settings only was the other option.
     26. **An agent's HTML file opens wherever it was written,** inside the project or not, as `marshell open` allows for any path. Only inside the project folder was the other option.
